. Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa.

Homage to the Blessed One, the Worthy One, the Fully Enlightened One.

Aṅguttaranikāyo

The Numerical Discourses

Aṭṭhakanipātapāḷi

The Book of the Eights

1. Paṭhamapaṇṇāsakaṃ

1. The First Fifty

1. Mettāvaggo

1. The Chapter on Loving-Kindness

1. Mettāsuttaṃ

1. The Discourse on Loving-Kindness

1. Evaṃ [T 4.0151][V 3.0001][P 4.0150][M 3.0001] me sutaṃ – ekaṃ samayaṃ bhagavā sāvatthiyaṃ viharati jetavane anāthapiṇḍikassa ārāme. Tatra kho bhagavā bhikkhū āmantesi – “bhikkhavo”ti. “Bhadante”ti te bhikkhū bhagavato paccassosuṃ. Bhagavā etadavoca –

1. Thus have I heard. On one occasion the Blessed One was dwelling at Sāvatthī in Jeta's Grove, Anāthapiṇḍika's monastery. There the Blessed One addressed the bhikkhus: “Bhikkhus!” “Venerable sir,” those bhikkhus replied to the Blessed One. The Blessed One said this:

a. ni. 11.15 “Mettāya, bhikkhave, cetovimuttiyā āsevitāya bhāvitāya bahulīkatāya yānīkatāya vatthukatāya [V 3.0002] anuṭṭhitāya paricitāya susamāraddhāya aṭṭhānisaṃsā pāṭikaṅkhā. Katame aṭṭha? Sukhaṃ supati, sukhaṃ paṭibujjhati, na pāpakaṃ supinaṃ passati, manussānaṃ piyo hoti, amanussānaṃ piyo hoti, devatā rakkhanti, nāssa aggi vā visaṃ vā satthaṃ vā kamati, uttariṃ appaṭivijjhanto brahmalokūpago hoti. Mettāya, bhikkhave, cetovimuttiyā āsevitāya bhāvitāya bahulīkatāya yānīkatāya vatthukatāya anuṭṭhitāya paricitāya susamāraddhāya ime aṭṭhānisaṃsā pāṭikaṅkhā”ti.

“Bhikkhus, when the liberation of mind through loving-kindness is practiced, developed, cultivated, made a vehicle, made a foundation, established, consolidated, and well undertaken, eight benefits are to be expected. What eight? One sleeps happily, one wakes happily, one does not see a bad dream, one is dear to human beings, one is dear to non-human beings, deities protect one, fire, poison, or a weapon does not affect one, and if one does not penetrate further, one is reborn in the Brahma world. Bhikkhus, when the liberation of mind through loving-kindness is practiced, developed, cultivated, made a vehicle, made a foundation, established, consolidated, and well undertaken, these eight benefits are to be expected.”

“Yo [T 4.0152][M 3.0002] ca mettaṃ bhāvayati, appamāṇaṃ paṭissato patissato (sī.);

Tanū saṃyojanā honti, passato upadhikkhayaṃ.

“Whoever, being mindful, develops boundless loving-kindness; for one who sees the destruction of the substrates, the fetters become attenuated.”

“Ekampi [P 4.0151] ce pāṇamaduṭṭhacitto,

Mettāyati kusalī tena hoti;

Sabbe ca pāṇe manasānukampī,

Pahūtamariyo pakaroti puññaṃ.

“If, with a mind not corrupted by ill will, one extends loving-kindness to even one living being, through that one becomes meritorious. The noble one, being compassionate in mind towards all beings, makes abundant merit.”

“Ye sattasaṇḍaṃ pathaviṃ vijetvā,

Rājisayo yajamānā anupariyagā;

Assamedhaṃ purisamedhaṃ,

Sammāpāsaṃ vājapeyyaṃ niraggaḷaṃ.

“Those kingly sages who, having conquered the earth with its multitudes of beings, traveled around making offerings unobstructedly: the horse sacrifice, the human sacrifice, the Sammāpāsa sacrifice, and the Vājapeyya sacrifice.”

“Mettassa cittassa subhāvitassa,

Kalampi te nānubhavanti soḷasiṃ;

Candappabhā tāragaṇāva sabbe,

Yathā na agghanti kalampi soḷasiṃ ayaṃ pādo bahūsu na dissati.

“These do not amount to a sixteenth part of a mind well-developed with loving-kindness, just as all the hosts of stars do not amount to a sixteenth part of the moon’s radiance.”

“Yo na hanti na ghāteti, na jināti na jāpaye;

Mettaṃso sabbabhūtānaṃ, veraṃ tassa na kenacī”ti. paṭhamaṃ;

“One who does not kill nor cause to kill, does not conquer nor cause to conquer, having a share of loving-kindness for all beings, has enmity with no one.” The first is finished.

2. Paññāsuttaṃ

2. The Discourse on Wisdom

2. “Aṭṭhime[V 3.0003], bhikkhave, hetū aṭṭha paccayā ādibrahmacariyikāya paññāya appaṭiladdhāya paṭilābhāya, paṭiladdhāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā saṃvattanti. Katame aṭṭha? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu satthāraṃ upanissāya viharati aññataraṃ vā garuṭṭhāniyaṃ sabrahmacāriṃ, yatthassa tibbaṃ hirottappaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ hoti pemañca gāravo ca. Ayaṃ[T 4.0153], bhikkhave, paṭhamo hetu paṭhamo paccayo ādibrahmacariyikāya paññāya appaṭiladdhāya paṭilābhāya, paṭiladdhāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā saṃvattati.

2. “Bhikkhus, these eight causes and eight conditions conduce to the acquisition of the wisdom fundamental to the holy life that has not yet been acquired, and to the increase, abundance, development, and fulfillment of that wisdom once it has been acquired. What are the eight? Here, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu dwells depending on the Teacher or on some fellow monk in a position of respect, towards whom he has established a keen sense of moral shame and moral dread, as well as affection and reverence. This, bhikkhus, is the first cause and first condition that conduces to the acquisition of the wisdom fundamental to the holy life that has not yet been acquired, and to the increase, abundance, development, and fulfillment of that wisdom once it has been acquired.”

“So [P 4.0152] taṃ satthāraṃ upanissāya viharanto aññataraṃ vā garuṭṭhāniyaṃ sabrahmacāriṃ, yatthassa tibbaṃ hirottappaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ hoti pemaṃ gāravo [M 3.0003] ca, te kālena kālaṃ upasaṅkamitvā paripucchati paripañhati – ‘idaṃ, bhante, kathaṃ; imassa ko attho’ti? Tassa te āyasmanto avivaṭañceva vivaranti, anuttānīkatañca uttānī karonti, anekavihitesu ca kaṅkhāṭhāniyesu dhammesu kaṅkhaṃ paṭivinodenti. Ayaṃ, bhikkhave, dutiyo hetu dutiyo paccayo ādibrahmacariyikāya paññāya appaṭiladdhāya paṭilābhāya, paṭiladdhāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā saṃvattati.

“Dwelling in dependence on that Teacher or on some fellow monk in a position of respect, towards whom he has established a keen sense of moral shame and moral dread, as well as affection and reverence, he approaches them from time to time to ask and to question: ‘Venerable sir, how is this? What is the meaning of this?’ Those venerable ones reveal to him what was not revealed, make clear what was not made clear, and dispel his doubt concerning many varied matters that are grounds for doubt. This, bhikkhus, is the second cause and second condition that conduces to the acquisition of the wisdom fundamental to the holy life that has not yet been acquired, and to the increase, abundance, development, and fulfillment of that wisdom once it has been acquired.”

“So taṃ dhammaṃ sutvā dvayena vūpakāsena sampādeti – kāyavūpakāsena ca cittavūpakāsena ca. Ayaṃ, bhikkhave, tatiyo hetu tatiyo paccayo ādibrahmacariyikāya paññāya appaṭiladdhāya paṭilābhāya, paṭiladdhāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā saṃvattati.

“Having heard that Dhamma, he accomplishes a twofold seclusion: seclusion of the body and seclusion of the mind. This, bhikkhus, is the third cause and third condition that conduces to the acquisition of the wisdom fundamental to the holy life that has not yet been acquired, and to the increase, abundance, development, and fulfillment of that wisdom once it has been acquired.”

“Sīlavā hoti, pātimokkhasaṃvarasaṃvuto viharati ācāragocarasampanno aṇumattesu vajjesu bhayadassāvī, samādāya sikkhati sikkhāpadesu. Ayaṃ, bhikkhave, catuttho hetu catuttho paccayo ādibrahmacariyikāya paññāya appaṭiladdhāya paṭilābhāya, paṭiladdhāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā saṃvattati.

“He is virtuous; he dwells restrained by the Pātimokkha, endowed with right conduct and resort, seeing danger in the slightest faults. He undertakes and trains in the training rules. This, bhikkhus, is the fourth cause and fourth condition that conduces to the acquisition of the wisdom fundamental to the holy life that has not yet been acquired, and to the increase, abundance, development, and fulfillment of that wisdom once it has been acquired.”

“Bahussuto hoti sutadharo sutasannicayo. Ye te dhammā ādikalyāṇā majjhekalyāṇā pariyosānakalyāṇā [V 3.0004] sātthaṃ sabyañjanaṃ [T 4.0154] satthā sabyañjanā (ka. sī.) kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ abhivadanti, tathārūpāssa dhammā bahussutā honti dhātā dhatā (sī. syā. kaṃ. pī.) vacasā paricitā manasānupekkhitā diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā. Ayaṃ, bhikkhave, pañcamo hetu pañcamo paccayo ādibrahmacariyikāya paññāya appaṭiladdhāya paṭilābhāya, paṭiladdhāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā saṃvattati.

“He is learned, a bearer of what is heard, an accumulator of what is heard. Those teachings that are good in the beginning, good in the middle, and good in the end, with their meaning and their phrasing, which proclaim the holy life that is utterly complete and pure—such teachings have been much heard by him, retained, familiarized by recitation, reflected upon by the mind, and well penetrated by view. This, bhikkhus, is the fifth cause and fifth condition that conduces to the acquisition of the wisdom fundamental to the holy life that has not yet been acquired, and to the increase, abundance, development, and fulfillment of that wisdom once it has been acquired.”

“Āraddhavīriyo [P 4.0153] viharati akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya, kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ upasampadāya, thāmavā daḷhaparakkamo anikkhittadhuro kusalesu dhammesu. Ayaṃ, bhikkhave, chaṭṭho hetu chaṭṭho paccayo ādibrahmacariyikāya paññāya appaṭiladdhāya paṭilābhāya, paṭiladdhāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā saṃvattati.

“He dwells with aroused energy for the abandoning of unwholesome states and for the acquisition of wholesome states; he is strong, steadfast in exertion, not shirking the burden in regard to wholesome states. This, bhikkhus, is the sixth cause and sixth condition that conduces to the acquisition of the wisdom fundamental to the holy life that has not yet been acquired, and to the increase, abundance, development, and fulfillment of that wisdom once it has been acquired.”

“Saṅghagato [M 3.0004] kho pana anānākathiko hoti atiracchānakathiko. Sāmaṃ vā dhammaṃ bhāsati paraṃ vā ajjhesati ariyaṃ vā tuṇhībhāvaṃ nātimaññati. Ayaṃ, bhikkhave, sattamo hetu sattamo paccayo ādibrahmacariyikāya paññāya appaṭiladdhāya paṭilābhāya, paṭiladdhāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā saṃvattati.

Furthermore, when in the midst of the Saṅgha, one is not of diverse talk, nor of talk that is contrary to the path and fruition. One either speaks the Dhamma oneself, or requests another, or does not disrespect the noble silence. This, bhikkhus, is the seventh generative cause, the seventh supporting condition for the acquisition of the insight-wisdom which is the beginning of the noble practice that has not yet been acquired, and for the increase, expansion, development, and fulfillment of the wisdom that has been acquired.

“Pañcasu kho pana upādānakkhandhesu udayabbayānupassī viharati – ‘iti rūpaṃ, iti rūpassa samudayo, iti rūpassa atthaṅgamo; iti vedanā, iti vedanāya samudayo, iti vedanāya atthaṅgamo; iti saññā…pe… iti saṅkhārā…pe… iti viññāṇaṃ, iti viññāṇassa samudayo, iti viññāṇassa atthaṅgamo’ti. Ayaṃ, bhikkhave, aṭṭhamo hetu aṭṭhamo paccayo ādibrahmacariyikāya paññāya appaṭiladdhāya paṭilābhāya, paṭiladdhāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā saṃvattati.

Furthermore, one dwells contemplating arising and passing away in the five aggregates of clinging: ‘Such is form, such is the arising of form, such is the cessation of form; such is feeling, such is the arising of feeling, such is the cessation of feeling; such is perception… such are formations… such is consciousness, such is the arising of consciousness, such is the cessation of consciousness.’ This, bhikkhus, is the eighth generative cause, the eighth supporting condition for the acquisition of the insight-wisdom which is the beginning of the noble practice that has not yet been acquired, and for the increase, expansion, development, and fulfillment of the wisdom that has been acquired.

“Tamenaṃ [T 4.0155] sabrahmacārī evaṃ sambhāventi – ‘ayaṃ kho āyasmā satthāraṃ upanissāya viharati aññataraṃ vā garuṭṭhāniyaṃ sabrahmacāriṃ, yatthassa tibbaṃ hirottappaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ hoti pemañca gāravo ca. Addhā ayamāyasmā jānaṃ jānāti passaṃ passatī’ti! Ayampi dhammo piyattāya garuttāya piyatāya garutāya (syā.) bhāvanāya sāmaññāya ekībhāvāya saṃvattati.

His fellows in the holy life esteem him thus: ‘Indeed, this venerable one dwells depending on the Teacher or on a certain fellow in the holy life who is in a position deserving of high esteem, towards whom intense shame and fear of wrongdoing, affection, and reverence are established for him. Assuredly, this venerable one, knowing, knows; seeing, sees!’ This quality also conduces to being beloved, to being esteemed, to development, to the state of a recluse, and to solitude.

“‘Taṃ [V 3.0005] kho panāyamāyasmā satthāraṃ upanissāya viharanto aññataraṃ vā garuṭṭhāniyaṃ sabrahmacāriṃ, yatthassa tibbaṃ hirottappaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ hoti pemañca [P 4.0154] gāravo ca, te kālena kālaṃ upasaṅkamitvā paripucchati paripañhati – idaṃ, bhante, kathaṃ; imassa ko atthoti? Tassa te āyasmanto avivaṭañceva vivaranti, anuttānīkatañca uttānī karonti, anekavihitesu ca kaṅkhāṭhāniyesu dhammesu kaṅkhaṃ paṭivinodenti. Addhā ayamāyasmā jānaṃ jānāti passaṃ passatī’ti! Ayampi dhammo piyattāya garuttāya bhāvanāya sāmaññāya ekībhāvāya saṃvattati.

‘Furthermore, this venerable one, while dwelling in dependence on the Teacher or a certain fellow in the holy life who is in a position deserving of high esteem, towards whom intense shame and fear of wrongdoing, affection, and reverence are established for him, approaches them from time to time, asks and inquires: “Venerable sir, how is this? What is the meaning of this?” Those venerable ones reveal to him what has not been revealed, make plain what has not been made plain, and dispel doubt in various matters that are grounds for doubt. Assuredly, this venerable one, knowing, knows; seeing, sees!’ This quality also conduces to being beloved, to being esteemed, to development, to the state of a recluse, and to solitude.

“‘Taṃ kho panāyamāyasmā dhammaṃ sutvā dvayena vūpakāsena sampādeti – kāyavūpakāsena ca cittavūpakāsena ca. Addhā ayamāyasmā jānaṃ jānāti passaṃ passatī’ti! Ayampi dhammo piyattāya garuttāya bhāvanāya sāmaññāya ekībhāvāya saṃvattati.

‘Furthermore, this venerable one, having heard that Dhamma, accomplishes a twofold seclusion: by means of bodily seclusion and by means of mental seclusion. Assuredly, this venerable one, knowing, knows; seeing, sees!’ This quality also conduces to being beloved, to being esteemed, to development, to the state of a recluse, and to solitude.

“‘Sīlavā [M 3.0005] kho panāyamāyasmā pātimokkhasaṃvarasaṃvuto viharati ācāragocarasampanno aṇumattesu vajjesu bhayadassāvī, samādāya sikkhati sikkhāpadesu. Addhā ayamāyasmā jānaṃ jānāti passaṃ passatī’ti! Ayampi dhammo piyattāya garuttāya bhāvanāya sāmaññāya ekībhāvāya saṃvattati.

‘Furthermore, this venerable one is virtuous; he dwells restrained by the Pātimokkha restraint, is accomplished in conduct and resort, and sees danger in the slightest faults; having undertaken them, he trains in the training rules. Assuredly, this venerable one, knowing, knows; seeing, sees!’ This quality also conduces to being beloved, to being esteemed, to development, to the state of a recluse, and to solitude.

“‘Bahussuto kho panāyamāyasmā [T 4.0156] sutadharo sutasannicayo. Ye te dhammā ādikalyāṇā majjhekalyāṇā pariyosānakalyāṇā sātthaṃ sabyañjanaṃ kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ abhivadanti, tathārūpāssa dhammā bahussutā honti dhātā vacasā paricitā manasānupekkhitā diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā. Addhā ayamāyasmā jānaṃ jānāti passaṃ passatī’ti! Ayampi dhammo piyattāya garuttāya bhāvanāya sāmaññāya ekībhāvāya saṃvattati.

‘Furthermore, this venerable one is very learned, a bearer of what is heard, an accumulator of what is heard. Those teachings that are good in the beginning, good in the middle, and good in the end, complete in meaning and phrasing, which proclaim the noble practice that is utterly complete and pure—such teachings have been much heard by him, are borne in mind, are practiced with the voice, are repeatedly contemplated with the mind, and are well penetrated by wisdom. Assuredly, this venerable one, knowing, knows; seeing, sees!’ This quality also conduces to being beloved, to being esteemed, to development, to the state of a recluse, and to solitude.

“‘Āraddhavīriyo kho panāyamāyasmā viharati akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya, kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ upasampadāya, thāmavā daḷhaparakkamo anikkhittadhuro kusalesu dhammesu. Addhā ayamāyasmā jānaṃ jānāti passaṃ passatī’ti! Ayampi dhammo piyattāya garuttāya bhāvanāya sāmaññāya ekībhāvāya saṃvattati.

‘Furthermore, this venerable one dwells with energy aroused for the abandoning of unwholesome states and for the accomplishment of wholesome states; he is strong, of firm endeavor, not having laid down the burden in regard to wholesome states. Assuredly, this venerable one, knowing, knows; seeing, sees!’ This quality also conduces to being beloved, to being esteemed, to development, to the state of a recluse, and to solitude.

“‘Saṅghagato [P 4.0155] kho panāyamāyasmā anānākathiko hoti atiracchānakathiko. Sāmaṃ vā dhammaṃ [V 3.0006] bhāsati paraṃ vā ajjhesati ariyaṃ vā tuṇhībhāvaṃ nātimaññati. Addhā ayamāyasmā jānaṃ jānāti passaṃ passatī’ti! Ayampi dhammo piyattāya garuttāya bhāvanāya sāmaññāya ekībhāvāya saṃvattati.

‘Furthermore, this venerable one, when in the midst of the Saṅgha, is not of diverse talk, nor of talk that is contrary to the path and fruition. He either speaks the Dhamma himself, or requests another, or does not disrespect the noble silence. Assuredly, this venerable one, knowing, knows; seeing, sees!’ This quality also conduces to being beloved, to being esteemed, to development, to the state of a recluse, and to solitude.

“‘Pañcasu kho panāyamāyasmā upādānakkhandhesu udayabbayānupassī viharati – iti rūpaṃ, iti rūpassa samudayo, iti rūpassa atthaṅgamo; iti vedanā…pe… iti saññā…pe… iti saṅkhārā…pe… iti viññāṇaṃ, iti viññāṇassa samudayo, iti viññāṇassa atthaṅgamoti. Addhā ayamāyasmā jānaṃ jānāti passaṃ passatī’ti! Ayampi dhammo piyattāya garuttāya bhāvanāya sāmaññāya ekībhāvāya saṃvattati.

‘Furthermore, this venerable one dwells contemplating arising and passing away in the five aggregates of clinging: “Such is form, such is the arising of form, such is the cessation of form; such is feeling… such is perception… such are formations… such is consciousness, such is the arising of consciousness, such is the cessation of consciousness.” Assuredly, this venerable one, knowing, knows; seeing, sees!’ This quality also conduces to being beloved, to being esteemed, to development, to the state of a recluse, and to solitude.

“Ime kho, bhikkhave, aṭṭha hetū aṭṭha paccayā [T 4.0157] ādibrahmacariyikāya paññāya appaṭiladdhāya paṭilābhāya, paṭiladdhāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā saṃvattantī”ti. Dutiyaṃ.

Indeed, bhikkhus, these are the eight generative causes, the eight supporting conditions that conduce to the acquisition of the insight-wisdom which is the beginning of the noble practice that has not yet been acquired, and for the increase, expansion, development, and fulfillment of the wisdom that has been acquired. The Second.

3. Paṭhamaappiyasuttaṃ

3. First Discourse on the Unloved

3. “Aṭṭhahi[M 3.0006], bhikkhave, dhammehi samannāgato bhikkhu sabrahmacārīnaṃ appiyo ca hoti amanāpo ca agaru ca abhāvanīyo ca. Katamehi aṭṭhahi? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu appiyapasaṃsī ca hoti, piyagarahī ca, lābhakāmo ca, sakkārakāmo ca, ahiriko ca, anottappī ca, pāpiccho ca, micchādiṭṭhi ca. Imehi kho, bhikkhave, aṭṭhahi dhammehi samannāgato bhikkhu sabrahmacārīnaṃ appiyo ca hoti amanāpo ca agaru ca abhāvanīyo ca.

3. “Monks, a monk endowed with eight qualities is unloved, disagreeable, unrespected, and not held in high regard by his companions in the holy life. With which eight? Here, monks, a monk praises the unloved and criticizes the loved; he is desirous of gain and desirous of honor; he is shameless and without moral dread; he has evil wishes and has wrong view. Monks, a monk endowed with these eight qualities is indeed unloved, disagreeable, unrespected, and not held in high regard by his companions in the holy life.”

“Aṭṭhahi, bhikkhave, dhammehi samannāgato bhikkhu sabrahmacārīnaṃ piyo ca hoti manāpo ca garu ca bhāvanīyo ca. Katamehi aṭṭhahi? Idha[P 4.0156], bhikkhave, bhikkhu na appiyapasaṃsī ca hoti, na piyagarahī ca, na lābhakāmo ca, na sakkārakāmo ca, hirīmā ca hoti, ottappī ca, appiccho ca, sammādiṭṭhi ca. Imehi kho, bhikkhave, aṭṭhahi dhammehi samannāgato [V 3.0007] bhikkhu sabrahmacārīnaṃ piyo ca hoti manāpo ca garu ca bhāvanīyo cā”ti. Tatiyaṃ.

“Monks, a monk endowed with eight qualities is loved, agreeable, respected, and held in high regard by his companions in the holy life. With which eight? Here, monks, a monk does not praise the unloved, nor does he criticize the loved; he is not desirous of gain, nor desirous of honor; he has moral shame and moral dread; he has few wishes and has right view. Monks, a monk endowed with these eight qualities is indeed loved, agreeable, respected, and held in high regard by his companions in the holy life.” The third.

4. Dutiyaappiyasuttaṃ

4. The Second Discourse on the Unloved

4. “Aṭṭhahi, bhikkhave, dhammehi samannāgato bhikkhu sabrahmacārīnaṃ appiyo ca hoti amanāpo ca agaru ca abhāvanīyo ca. Katamehi aṭṭhahi? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu lābhakāmo ca hoti, sakkārakāmo ca, anavaññattikāmo ca, akālaññū ca, amattaññū ca, asuci ca, bahubhāṇī ca, akkosakaparibhāsako ca sabrahmacārīnaṃ. Imehi kho, bhikkhave, aṭṭhahi dhammehi samannāgato bhikkhu sabrahmacārīnaṃ appiyo ca hoti amanāpo ca[T 4.0158] agaru ca abhāvanīyo ca.

4. “Monks, a monk endowed with eight qualities is unloved, disagreeable, unrespected, and not held in high regard by his companions in the holy life. With which eight? Here, monks, a monk is desirous of gain, desirous of honor, and desirous of not being despised; he does not know the proper time, does not know moderation, is impure, and is talkative; and he is one who insults and reviles his companions in the holy life. Monks, a monk endowed with these eight qualities is indeed unloved, disagreeable, unrespected, and not held in high regard by his companions in the holy life.”

“Aṭṭhahi, bhikkhave, dhammehi samannāgato bhikkhu sabrahmacārīnaṃ piyo ca hoti manāpo ca garu ca bhāvanīyo ca. Katamehi aṭṭhahi? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu na lābhakāmo ca hoti, na sakkārakāmo ca, na anavaññattikāmo ca, kālaññū ca, mattaññū ca, suci ca, na bahubhāṇī ca, anakkosakaparibhāsako ca sabrahmacārīnaṃ. Imehi kho, bhikkhave, aṭṭhahi dhammehi samannāgato bhikkhu sabrahmacārīnaṃ piyo ca hoti manāpo ca garu ca bhāvanīyo cā”ti. Catutthaṃ.

“Monks, a monk endowed with eight qualities is loved, agreeable, respected, and held in high regard by his companions in the holy life. With which eight? Here, monks, a monk is not desirous of gain, not desirous of honor, and not desirous of not being despised; he knows the proper time, knows moderation, is pure, and is not talkative; and he is one who does not insult or revile his companions in the holy life. Monks, a monk endowed with these eight qualities is indeed loved, agreeable, respected, and held in high regard by his companions in the holy life.” The fourth.

5. Paṭhamalokadhammasuttaṃ

5. The First Discourse on Worldly Conditions

5. “Aṭṭhime[M 3.0007], bhikkhave, lokadhammā lokaṃ anuparivattanti, loko ca aṭṭha lokadhamme anuparivattati. Katame aṭṭha? Lābho [P 4.0157] ca, alābho ca, yaso ca, ayaso ca, nindā ca, pasaṃsā ca, sukhañca, dukkhañca. Ime kho, bhikkhave, aṭṭha lokadhammā lokaṃ anuparivattanti, loko ca ime aṭṭha lokadhamme anuparivattatī”ti.

5. “Monks, these eight worldly conditions revolve around the world, and the world revolves around these eight worldly conditions. What are the eight? Gain and loss, fame and disgrace, censure and praise, pleasure and pain. These, monks, are the eight worldly conditions that revolve around the world, and the world revolves around these eight worldly conditions.”

“Lābho alābho ca yasāyaso ca,

Nindā pasaṃsā ca sukhaṃ dukhañca;

Ete [V 3.0008] aniccā manujesu dhammā,

Asassatā vipariṇāmadhammā.

“Gain and loss, fame and disgrace,

Censure and praise, pleasure and pain;

These conditions among human beings are impermanent,

Not lasting, and subject to change.

“Ete ca ñatvā satimā sumedho,

Avekkhati vipariṇāmadhamme;

Iṭṭhassa dhammā na mathenti cittaṃ,

Aniṭṭhato no paṭighātameti.

“Having known these, the mindful one, the one of good wisdom,

Contemplates them as being subject to change;

Desirable conditions do not agitate his mind,

And he feels no resistance toward the undesirable.

“Tassānurodhā [T 4.0159] atha vā virodhā,

Vidhūpitā atthaṅgatā na santi;

Padañca ñatvā virajaṃ asokaṃ,

Sammappajānāti bhavassa pāragū”ti. pañcamaṃ;

“For him, both conformity and opposition

Are dispelled, have vanished, and exist no more.

Having known the dustless, sorrowless state,

He who has gone to the far shore of existence rightly understands.” The fifth.

6. Dutiyalokadhammasuttaṃ

6. The Second Discourse on Worldly Conditions

6. “Aṭṭhime, bhikkhave, lokadhammā lokaṃ anuparivattanti, loko ca aṭṭha lokadhamme anuparivattati. Katame aṭṭha? Lābho ca, alābho ca, yaso ca, ayaso ca, nindā ca, pasaṃsā ca, sukhañca, dukkhañca. Ime kho, bhikkhave, aṭṭha lokadhammā lokaṃ anuparivattanti, loko ca ime aṭṭha lokadhamme anuparivattati.

6. “Monks, these eight worldly conditions revolve around the world, and the world revolves around these eight worldly conditions. What are the eight? Gain and loss, fame and disgrace, censure and praise, pleasure and pain. These, monks, are the eight worldly conditions that revolve around the world, and the world revolves around these eight worldly conditions.

“Assutavato, bhikkhave, puthujjanassa uppajjati lābhopi alābhopi yasopi ayasopi nindāpi pasaṃsāpi sukhampi dukkhampi. Sutavatopi, bhikkhave, ariyasāvakassa uppajjati lābhopi alābhopi yasopi ayasopi nindāpi pasaṃsāpi sukhampi dukkhampi. Tatra, bhikkhave, ko viseso [P 4.0158] ko [M 3.0008] adhippayāso adhippāyo (sī.), adhippāyaso (syā. kaṃ.) adhi + pa + yasu + ṇa = adhippayāso kiṃ nānākaraṇaṃ sutavato ariyasāvakassa assutavatā puthujjanenā”ti? “Bhagavaṃmūlakā no, bhante, dhammā bhagavaṃnettikā bhagavaṃpaṭisaraṇā. Sādhu vata, bhante, bhagavantaṃyeva paṭibhātu etassa bhāsitassa attho. Bhagavato sutvā bhikkhū dhāressantī”ti.

“Monks, for an unlearned ordinary person, there arise gain and loss, fame and disgrace, censure and praise, pleasure and pain. For a learned noble disciple, monks, there also arise gain and loss, fame and disgrace, censure and praise, pleasure and pain. In that case, monks, what is the distinction, what is the divergence, what is the difference for the learned noble disciple from the unlearned ordinary person?” “Venerable sir, our teachings have the Blessed One as their root, the Blessed One as their guide, the Blessed One as their refuge. It would be good, venerable sir, if the meaning of this statement would be illuminated by the Blessed One himself. Having heard it from the Blessed One, the monks will bear it in mind.”

“Tena [V 3.0009] hi, bhikkhave, suṇātha, sādhukaṃ manasi karotha; bhāsissāmī”ti. “Evaṃ, bhante”ti kho te bhikkhū bhagavato paccassosuṃ. Bhagavā etadavoca – “assutavato, bhikkhave, puthujjanassa uppajjati lābho. So na iti paṭisañcikkhati – ‘uppanno kho me ayaṃ lābho; so ca kho anicco dukkho vipariṇāmadhammo’ti [T 4.0160] yathābhūtaṃ nappajānāti. Uppajjati alābho…pe… uppajjati yaso… uppajjati ayaso… uppajjati nindā… uppajjati pasaṃsā… uppajjati sukhaṃ… uppajjati dukkhaṃ. So na iti paṭisañcikkhati – ‘uppannaṃ kho me idaṃ dukkhaṃ; tañca kho aniccaṃ dukkhaṃ vipariṇāmadhamma’nti yathābhūtaṃ nappajānāti”.

“In that case, monks, listen and apply your minds well; I will speak.” “Yes, venerable sir,” those monks replied to the Blessed One. The Blessed One said this: “Monks, for an unlearned ordinary person, gain arises. He does not reflect thus: ‘This gain has arisen for me; and it is impermanent, suffering, and subject to change.’ He does not understand it as it really is. Loss arises… fame arises… disgrace arises… censure arises… praise arises… pleasure arises… pain arises. He does not reflect thus: ‘This pain has arisen for me; and it is impermanent, suffering, and subject to change.’ He does not understand it as it really is.”

“Tassa lābhopi cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, alābhopi cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, yasopi cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, ayasopi cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, nindāpi cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, pasaṃsāpi cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, sukhampi cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, dukkhampi cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhati. So uppannaṃ lābhaṃ anurujjhati, alābhe paṭivirujjhati; uppannaṃ yasaṃ anurujjhati, ayase paṭivirujjhati; uppannaṃ pasaṃsaṃ anurujjhati, nindāya paṭivirujjhati; uppannaṃ sukhaṃ anurujjhati, dukkhe paṭivirujjhati. So evaṃ anurodhavirodhasamāpanno na parimuccati jātiyā jarāya maraṇena sokehi paridevehi dukkhehi domanassehi upāyāsehi. ‘Na parimuccati dukkhasmā’ti vadāmi”.

“Gain overwhelms his mind and remains, loss overwhelms his mind and remains, fame overwhelms his mind and remains, disgrace overwhelms his mind and remains, censure overwhelms his mind and remains, praise overwhelms his mind and remains, pleasure overwhelms his mind and remains, and pain overwhelms his mind and remains. He delights in the gain that has arisen and resists loss; he delights in the fame that has arisen and resists disgrace; he delights in the praise that has arisen and resists censure; he delights in the pleasure that has arisen and resists pain. Being one who has fallen into conformity and opposition in this way, he is not freed from birth, from aging, from death, from sorrows, lamentations, pains, griefs, and despairs. He is not freed from suffering, I say.”

“Sutavato ca kho, bhikkhave, ariyasāvakassa uppajjati lābho. So iti paṭisañcikkhati – ‘uppanno kho me ayaṃ lābho; so ca kho anicco dukkho vipariṇāmadhammo’ti yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti. Uppajjati alābho…pe… uppajjati yaso… uppajjati [P 4.0159] ayaso… uppajjati nindā… uppajjati pasaṃsā… uppajjati sukhaṃ… uppajjati dukkhaṃ. So iti paṭisañcikkhati – ‘uppannaṃ kho me idaṃ [T 4.0161] dukkhaṃ; tañca kho aniccaṃ dukkhaṃ vipariṇāmadhamma’nti yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti”.

“And for a learned noble disciple, bhikkhus, gain arises. He reflects thus: ‘This gain has arisen for me; and it is impermanent, suffering, and subject to change.’ He understands it as it really is. Loss arises… fame arises… disgrace arises… censure arises… praise arises… pleasure arises… pain arises. He reflects thus: ‘This pain has arisen for me; and it is impermanent, suffering, and subject to change.’ He understands it as it really is.”

“Tassa [M 3.0009] lābhopi cittaṃ na pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, alābhopi cittaṃ na pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, yasopi cittaṃ na pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, ayasopi cittaṃ na pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, nindāpi cittaṃ na pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, pasaṃsāpi cittaṃ na pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, sukhampi cittaṃ [V 3.0010] na pariyādāya tiṭṭhati, dukkhampi cittaṃ na pariyādāya tiṭṭhati. So uppannaṃ lābhaṃ nānurujjhati, alābhe nappaṭivirujjhati; uppannaṃ yasaṃ nānurujjhati, ayase nappaṭivirujjhati; uppannaṃ pasaṃsaṃ nānurujjhati, nindāya nappaṭivirujjhati; uppannaṃ sukhaṃ nānurujjhati, dukkhe nappaṭivirujjhati. So evaṃ anurodhavirodhavippahīno parimuccati jātiyā jarāya maraṇena sokehi paridevehi dukkhehi domanassehi upāyāsehi. ‘Parimuccati dukkhasmā’ti vadāmi. Ayaṃ kho, bhikkhave, viseso ayaṃ adhippayāso idaṃ nānākaraṇaṃ sutavato ariyasāvakassa assutavatā puthujjanenā”ti.

“For that noble disciple, gain does not consume his mind and remain, nor does loss consume his mind and remain; fame does not consume his mind and remain, nor does disgrace consume his mind and remain; censure does not consume his mind and remain, nor does praise consume his mind and remain; pleasure does not consume his mind and remain, nor does pain consume his mind and remain. He is not pleased with arisen gain, nor is he opposed to loss. He is not pleased with arisen fame, nor is he opposed to disgrace. He is not pleased with arisen praise, nor is he opposed to censure. He is not pleased with arisen pleasure, nor is he opposed to pain. Thus, having abandoned conformity and opposition, he is freed from birth, aging, and death, from sorrows, lamentations, pains, griefs, and despairs. ‘He is freed from suffering,’ I say. This, bhikkhus, is the distinction, this is the great exertion, this is the difference for an instructed noble disciple compared with an uninstructed ordinary person.”

“Lābho alābho ca yasāyaso ca,

Nindā pasaṃsā ca sukhaṃ dukhañca;

Ete aniccā manujesu dhammā,

Asassatā vipariṇāmadhammā.

“Gain and loss, fame and disgrace, censure and praise, and pleasure and pain—these eight conditions among humans are impermanent, not lasting, and subject to change.

“Ete ca ñatvā satimā sumedho,

Avekkhati vipariṇāmadhamme;

Iṭṭhassa [T 4.0162] dhammā na mathenti cittaṃ,

Aniṭṭhato no paṭighātameti.

“Having known these, the mindful and wise one reflects upon these things that are subject to change. Desirable things do not agitate his mind, nor does he come to vexation on account of the undesirable.

“Tassānurodhā [P 4.0160] atha vā virodhā,

Vidhūpitā atthaṅgatā na santi;

Padañca ñatvā virajaṃ asokaṃ,

Sammappajānāti bhavassa pāragū”ti. chaṭṭhaṃ;

“For him, conformity or opposition are dispelled, have vanished, and are no more. Having known the dustless, sorrowless state, the one who has gone to the far shore of existence rightly understands.” The Sixth.

7. Devadattavipattisuttaṃ

7. The Discourse on the Downfall of Devadatta

7. Ekaṃ samayaṃ bhagavā rājagahe viharati gijjhakūṭe pabbate acirapakkante devadatte. Tatra bhagavā devadattaṃ ārabbha bhikkhū āmantesi – “sādhu, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ attavipattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti. Sādhu, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ paravipattiṃ paccavekkhitā [V 3.0011] hoti. Sādhu, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ attasampattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti. Sādhu[M 3.0010], bhikkhave, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ parasampattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti. Aṭṭhahi, bhikkhave, asaddhammehi abhibhūto pariyādinnacitto devadatto āpāyiko nerayiko kappaṭṭho atekiccho”.

7. On one occasion the Blessed One was dwelling at Rājagaha on the Vulture Peak Mountain, not long after Devadatta had departed. There the Blessed One, concerning Devadatta, addressed the bhikkhus: “It is good, bhikkhus, for a bhikkhu from time to time to review his own downfall. It is good, bhikkhus, for a bhikkhu from time to time to review the downfall of others. It is good, bhikkhus, for a bhikkhu from time to time to review his own accomplishment. It is good, bhikkhus, for a bhikkhu from time to time to review the accomplishment of others. Bhikkhus, Devadatta, overwhelmed and with his mind consumed by eight unrighteous conditions, is bound for the plane of misery, bound for hell, to remain for an aeon, incurable.”

cūḷava. 348 “Katamehi aṭṭhahi? Lābhena hi, bhikkhave, abhibhūto pariyādinnacitto devadatto āpāyiko nerayiko kappaṭṭho atekiccho. Alābhena, bhikkhave…pe… yasena, bhikkhave… ayasena, bhikkhave… sakkārena, bhikkhave… asakkārena, bhikkhave… pāpicchatāya, bhikkhave… pāpamittatāya, bhikkhave, abhibhūto pariyādinnacitto devadatto āpāyiko nerayiko kappaṭṭho atekiccho. Imehi kho, bhikkhave, aṭṭhahi asaddhammehi abhibhūto pariyādinnacitto devadatto āpāyiko [T 4.0163] nerayiko kappaṭṭho atekiccho.

“By which eight? Bhikkhus, Devadatta, overwhelmed and with his mind consumed by gain, is bound for the plane of misery, bound for hell, to remain for an aeon, incurable. By loss, bhikkhus… by fame, bhikkhus… by disgrace, bhikkhus… by honor, bhikkhus… by dishonor, bhikkhus… by evil desire, bhikkhus… by evil friendship, bhikkhus, Devadatta, overwhelmed and with his mind consumed, is bound for the plane of misery, bound for hell, to remain for an aeon, incurable. Indeed, bhikkhus, Devadatta, overwhelmed and with his mind consumed by these eight unrighteous conditions, is bound for the plane of misery, bound for hell, to remain for an aeon, incurable.”

“Sādhu, bhikkhave, bhikkhu uppannaṃ lābhaṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya, uppannaṃ alābhaṃ…pe… uppannaṃ yasaṃ… uppannaṃ ayasaṃ… uppannaṃ sakkāraṃ [P 4.0161]… uppannaṃ asakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ pāpicchataṃ… uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya.

“It is good, bhikkhus, that a bhikkhu should dwell having repeatedly overcome arisen gain, having repeatedly overcome arisen loss… arisen fame… arisen disgrace… arisen honor… arisen dishonor… arisen evil desire… arisen evil friendship.”

“Kiñca kathañca (ka.), bhikkhave, bhikkhu atthavasaṃ paṭicca uppannaṃ lābhaṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya, uppannaṃ alābhaṃ…pe… uppannaṃ yasaṃ… uppannaṃ ayasaṃ… uppannaṃ sakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ asakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ pāpicchataṃ… uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya?

“And for what reason, bhikkhus, should a bhikkhu dwell having repeatedly overcome arisen gain, having repeatedly overcome arisen loss… arisen fame… arisen disgrace… arisen honor… arisen dishonor… arisen evil desire… arisen evil friendship?”

“Yaṃ hissa, bhikkhave, uppannaṃ lābhaṃ anabhibhuyya anabhibhūyya anabhibhūyya (ka.) viharato uppajjeyyuṃ āsavā vighātapariḷāhā, uppannaṃ lābhaṃ abhibhuyya abhibhūyya abhibhūyya (ka.) viharato evaṃsa te āsavā vighātapariḷāhā na honti. Yaṃ hissa, bhikkhave, uppannaṃ alābhaṃ…pe… uppannaṃ yasaṃ… uppannaṃ ayasaṃ… uppannaṃ sakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ asakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ pāpicchataṃ… uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ anabhibhuyya viharato uppajjeyyuṃ āsavā vighātapariḷāhā, uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ abhibhuyya viharato evaṃsa te āsavā vighātapariḷāhā na honti. Idaṃ kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu atthavasaṃ paṭicca uppannaṃ lābhaṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya, uppannaṃ alābhaṃ…pe… uppannaṃ yasaṃ… uppannaṃ ayasaṃ [V 3.0012] … uppannaṃ sakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ asakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ pāpicchataṃ… uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya.

“Because, monks, for one who dwells without overcoming arisen gain, there would arise taints of vexation and feverishness. But for one who dwells having overcome arisen gain, those taints of vexation and feverishness do not arise. Because, monks, for one who dwells without overcoming arisen loss… arisen fame… arisen disrepute… arisen honor… arisen dishonor… arisen evil desire… arisen evil friendship, there would arise taints of vexation and feverishness. But for one who dwells having overcome arisen evil friendship, those taints of vexation and feverishness do not arise. This, indeed, monks, is the reason a monk should dwell having repeatedly overcome arisen gain, having repeatedly overcome arisen loss… arisen fame… arisen disrepute… arisen honor… arisen dishonor… arisen evil desire… arisen evil friendship.”

“Tasmātiha[M 3.0011], bhikkhave, evaṃ sikkhitabbaṃ – ‘uppannaṃ lābhaṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya viharissāma, uppannaṃ alābhaṃ…pe… uppannaṃ yasaṃ… uppannaṃ ayasaṃ… uppannaṃ sakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ asakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ pāpicchataṃ… uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya viharissāmā’ti. Evañhi vo, bhikkhave, sikkhitabba”nti. Sattamaṃ.

“Therefore, monks, you should train thus: ‘We will dwell having repeatedly overcome arisen gain, having repeatedly overcome arisen loss… arisen fame… arisen disrepute… arisen honor… arisen dishonor… arisen evil desire… arisen evil friendship.’ Thus, monks, should you train.” The Seventh.

8. Uttaravipattisuttaṃ

8. The Uttara Discourse on Decline

8. Ekaṃ [T 4.0164][P 4.0162] samayaṃ āyasmā uttaro mahisavatthusmiṃ viharati saṅkheyyake pabbate vaṭajālikāyaṃ dhavajālikāyaṃ (sī.), vaṭṭajālikāyaṃ (syā.). Tatra kho āyasmā uttaro bhikkhū āmantesi – “sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ attavipattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti. Sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ paravipattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti. Sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ attasampattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti. Sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ parasampattiṃ paccavekkhitā hotī”ti.

8. On one occasion, the Venerable Uttara was dwelling at Mahisavatthu, on Saṅkheyya Mountain, in the Vaṭajālikā monastery. There, the Venerable Uttara addressed the monks thus: “Friends, it is good for a monk to reflect from time to time on his own decline. Friends, it is good for a monk to reflect from time to time on the decline of others. Friends, it is good for a monk to reflect from time to time on his own accomplishment. Friends, it is good for a monk to reflect from time to time on the accomplishment of others.”

Tena kho pana samayena vessavaṇo mahārājā uttarāya disāya dakkhiṇaṃ disaṃ gacchati kenacideva karaṇīyena. Assosi kho vessavaṇo mahārājā āyasmato uttarassa mahisavatthusmiṃ saṅkheyyake pabbate vaṭajālikāyaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ evaṃ dhammaṃ desentassa – “sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ attavipattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti. Sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ paravipattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti. Sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ attasampattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti. Sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ parasampattiṃ paccavekkhitā hotī”ti.

Now at that time, the great king Vessavaṇa, on some business or other, was traveling from the northern direction to the southern direction. The great king Vessavaṇa heard the sound of the Venerable Uttara at Mahisavatthu, on Saṅkheyya Mountain, in the Vaṭajālikā monastery, teaching the Dhamma to the monks thus: “Friends, it is good for a monk to reflect from time to time on his own decline. Friends, it is good for a monk to reflect from time to time on the decline of others. Friends, it is good for a monk to reflect from time to time on his own accomplishment. Friends, it is good for a monk to reflect from time to time on the accomplishment of others.”

Atha kho vessavaṇṇo mahārājā – seyyathāpi nāma balavā puriso samiñjitaṃ sammiñjitaṃ (sī. syā. kaṃ. pī.) vā bāhaṃ pasāreyya, pasāritaṃ vā bāhaṃ samiñjeyya sammiñjeyya (sī. syā. kaṃ. pī.), evamevaṃ mahisavatthusmiṃ saṅkheyyake pabbate vaṭajālikāyaṃ antarahito devesu tāvatiṃsesu pāturahosi. Atha kho vessavaṇṇo mahārājā yena sakko devānamindo tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā sakkaṃ [T 4.0165] devānamindaṃ etadavoca – “yagghe mārisa, jāneyyāsi! Eso āyasmā uttaro mahisavatthusmiṃ saṅkheyyake [P 4.0163] pabbate vaṭajālikāyaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ [V 3.0013] evaṃ dhammaṃ deseti – ‘sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ attavipattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti[M 3.0012]. Sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ paravipattiṃ…pe… attasampattiṃ… parasampattiṃ paccavekkhitā hotī’”ti.

Then the great king Vessavaṇa—just as a strong man might extend his flexed arm or flex his extended arm—disappeared from Mahisavatthu, on Saṅkheyya Mountain, in the Vaṭajālikā monastery and appeared among the Tāvatiṃsa devas. Then the great king Vessavaṇa approached Sakka, lord of the devas, and said to him: “If it pleases you, my lord, you should know this. The Venerable Uttara is teaching the Dhamma to the monks at Mahisavatthu, on Saṅkheyya Mountain, in the Vaṭajālikā monastery, in this way: ‘Friends, it is good for a monk to reflect from time to time on his own decline. Friends, it is good for a monk to reflect from time to time on the decline of others… on his own accomplishment… on the accomplishment of others.’”

Atha kho sakko devānamindo seyyathāpi nāma balavā puriso samiñjitaṃ vā bāhaṃ pasāreyya, pasāritaṃ vā bāhaṃ samiñjeyya, evamevaṃ devesu tāvatiṃsesu antarahito mahisavatthusmiṃ saṅkheyyake pabbate vaṭajālikāyaṃ āyasmato uttarassa sammukhe pāturahosi. Atha kho sakko devānamindo yenāyasmā uttaro tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā āyasmantaṃ uttaraṃ abhivādetvā ekamantaṃ aṭṭhāsi. Ekamantaṃ ṭhito kho sakko devānamindo āyasmantaṃ uttaraṃ etadavoca –

Then Sakka, lord of the devas—just as a strong man might extend his flexed arm or flex his extended arm—disappeared from among the Tāvatiṃsa devas and appeared in front of the Venerable Uttara at Mahisavatthu, on Saṅkheyya Mountain, in the Vaṭajālikā monastery. Then Sakka, lord of the devas, approached the Venerable Uttara, paid homage to him, and stood to one side. Standing to one side, Sakka, lord of the devas, said this to the Venerable Uttara:

“Saccaṃ kira, bhante, āyasmā uttaro bhikkhūnaṃ evaṃ dhammaṃ desesi – ‘sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ attavipattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti, sādhāvuso, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ paravipattiṃ…pe… attasampattiṃ… parasampattiṃ paccavekkhitā hotī’” ti? “Evaṃ, devānamindā”ti. “Kiṃ panidaṃ kiṃ pana (syā.), bhante, āyasmato uttarassa sakaṃ paṭibhānaṃ sakapaṭibhānaṃ upādāya (ka.), udāhu tassa bhagavato vacanaṃ arahato sammāsambuddhassā”ti? “Tena hi, devānaminda, upamaṃ te karissāmi. Upamāya midhekacce viññū purisā bhāsitassa atthaṃ ājāna”nti.

“Is it true, venerable sir, as has been reported, that the Venerable Uttara taught the Dhamma to the monks in this way: ‘Friends, it is good for a monk to reflect from time to time on his own decline… on the decline of others… on his own accomplishment… on the accomplishment of others’?” “It is so, Lord of Devas.” “But, venerable sir, is this the Venerable Uttara’s own inspiration, or is it the word of the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Enlightened One?” “Well then, Lord of Devas, I will give you a simile. For by means of a simile, some wise people in this world understand the meaning of what is said.”

“Seyyathāpi, devānaminda, gāmassa vā nigamassa vā avidūre mahādhaññarāsi. Tato mahājanakāyo dhaññaṃ āhareyya – kājehipi piṭakehipi ucchaṅgehipi [P 4.0164] añjalīhipi[T 4.0166]. Yo nu kho, devānaminda, taṃ mahājanakāyaṃ upasaṅkamitvā evaṃ puccheyya – ‘kuto imaṃ dhaññaṃ āharathā’ti, kathaṃ byākaramāno nu kho, devānaminda, so mahājanakāyo sammā byākaramāno byākareyyā”ti? “‘Amumhā mahādhaññarāsimhā āharāmā’ti kho, bhante, so mahājanakāyo sammā byākaramāno byākareyyā”ti. “Evamevaṃ kho, devānaminda, yaṃ kiñci subhāsitaṃ sabbaṃ taṃ tassa bhagavato vacanaṃ arahato sammāsambuddhassa. Tato upādāyupādāya mayaṃ caññe ca bhaṇāmā”ti.

“Suppose, Lord of Devas, there were a great heap of grain not far from a village or a town. From it, a great crowd of people would carry away grain—with carrying poles, in baskets, in their laps, and in their cupped hands. Now, Lord of Devas, if someone were to approach that great crowd of people and ask: ‘From where are you carrying away this grain?’—how, Lord of Devas, should that great crowd of people reply in order to answer correctly?” “Venerable sir, that great crowd of people, in order to answer correctly, should reply: ‘We are carrying it away from that great heap of grain.’” “Just so, Lord of Devas, whatever is well spoken, all that is the word of the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Enlightened One. Drawing from that source, we and others speak.”

“Acchariyaṃ, bhante, abbhutaṃ bhante! Yāva subhāsitaṃ cidaṃ āyasmatā uttarena – ‘yaṃ kiñci [V 3.0014] subhāsitaṃ sabbaṃ taṃ tassa bhagavato vacanaṃ arahato sammāsambuddhassa[M 3.0013]. Tato upādāyupādāya mayaṃ caññe ca bhaṇāmā’ti. Ekamidaṃ, bhante uttara, samayaṃ bhagavā rājagahe viharati gijjhakūṭe pabbate acirapakkante devadatte. Tatra kho bhagavā devadattaṃ ārabbha bhikkhū āmantesi –

“Wonderful, venerable sir! Marvelous, venerable sir! How well-spoken this is by the Venerable Uttara: ‘Whatever is well spoken, all that is the word of the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Enlightened One. Drawing from that source, we and others speak.’ On one occasion, Venerable Uttara, the Blessed One was dwelling at Rājagaha, on Vulture Peak Mountain, not long after Devadatta had departed. There, the Blessed One, concerning Devadatta, addressed the monks:”

“Sādhu, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ attavipattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti. Sādhu, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ paravipattiṃ…pe… attasampattiṃ… parasampattiṃ paccavekkhitā hoti. Aṭṭhahi, bhikkhave, asaddhammehi abhibhūto pariyādinnacitto devadatto āpāyiko nerayiko kappaṭṭho atekiccho. Katamehi aṭṭhahi? Lābhena hi, bhikkhave, abhibhūto pariyādinnacitto devadatto āpāyiko nerayiko kappaṭṭho atekiccho; alābhena, bhikkhave…pe… yasena, bhikkhave [T 4.0167]… ayasena, bhikkhave… sakkārena, bhikkhave… asakkārena, bhikkhave… pāpicchatāya, bhikkhave… pāpamittatāya[P 4.0165], bhikkhave, abhibhūto pariyādinnacitto devadatto āpāyiko nerayiko kappaṭṭho atekiccho. Imehi kho, bhikkhave, aṭṭhahi asaddhammehi abhibhūto pariyādinnacitto devadatto āpāyiko nerayiko kappaṭṭho atekiccho.

“It is good, bhikkhus, if a bhikkhu from time to time reflects on his own ruin. It is good, bhikkhus, if a bhikkhu from time to time reflects on the ruin of others… on his own accomplishment… on the accomplishment of others. Bhikkhus, Devadatta, overcome and with his mind consumed by eight unrighteous qualities, is destined for the plane of misery, destined for hell, to remain for the aeon, irremediable. By which eight? Bhikkhus, Devadatta, overcome and with his mind consumed by gain, is destined for the plane of misery, destined for hell, to remain for the aeon, irremediable; by lack of gain, bhikkhus… by fame, bhikkhus… by lack of fame, bhikkhus… by honor, bhikkhus… by lack of honor, bhikkhus… by evil desires, bhikkhus… by bad friendship, bhikkhus, Devadatta, overcome and with his mind consumed, is destined for the plane of misery, destined for hell, to remain for the aeon, irremediable. Indeed, bhikkhus, it is by these eight unrighteous qualities that Devadatta, overcome and with his mind consumed, is destined for the plane of misery, destined for hell, to remain for the aeon, irremediable.”

“Sādhu, bhikkhave, bhikkhu uppannaṃ lābhaṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya; uppannaṃ alābhaṃ…pe… uppannaṃ yasaṃ… uppannaṃ ayasaṃ… uppannaṃ sakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ asakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ pāpicchataṃ… uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya.

“It is good, bhikkhus, that a bhikkhu should dwell repeatedly overcoming any arisen gain; any arisen lack of gain… any arisen fame… any arisen lack of fame… any arisen honor… any arisen lack of honor… any arisen evil desires… and any arisen bad friendship.”

“Kiñca, bhikkhave, bhikkhu atthavasaṃ paṭicca uppannaṃ lābhaṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya; uppannaṃ alābhaṃ…pe… uppannaṃ yasaṃ… uppannaṃ ayasaṃ… uppannaṃ sakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ asakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ pāpicchataṃ… uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya?

“And for what reason, bhikkhus, should a bhikkhu dwell repeatedly overcoming any arisen gain; any arisen lack of gain… any arisen fame… any arisen lack of fame… any arisen honor… any arisen lack of honor… any arisen evil desires… and any arisen bad friendship?”

“Yaṃ hissa, bhikkhave, uppannaṃ lābhaṃ anabhibhuyya viharato uppajjeyyuṃ āsavā vighātapariḷāhā, uppannaṃ lābhaṃ abhibhuyya viharato evaṃsa te āsavā vighātapariḷāhā na honti. Yaṃ hissa, bhikkhave, uppannaṃ alābhaṃ…pe… uppannaṃ yasaṃ… uppannaṃ ayasaṃ… uppannaṃ sakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ asakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ pāpicchataṃ… uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ anabhibhuyya viharato [V 3.0015] uppajjeyyuṃ āsavā vighātapariḷāhā, uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ abhibhuyya viharato evaṃsa [M 3.0014] te āsavā vighātapariḷāhā na honti. Idaṃ kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu atthavasaṃ paṭicca uppannaṃ lābhaṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya; uppannaṃ alābhaṃ…pe… uppannaṃ yasaṃ… uppannaṃ ayasaṃ… uppannaṃ sakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ asakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ pāpicchataṃ [T 4.0168]… uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya vihareyya.

“Because, bhikkhus, for one who dwells without overcoming an arisen gain, taints of distress and fever would arise. For one who dwells overcoming an arisen gain, those taints of distress and fever do not arise. Because, bhikkhus, for one who dwells without overcoming an arisen lack of gain… arisen fame… arisen lack of fame… arisen honor… arisen lack of honor… arisen evil desires… or arisen bad friendship, taints of distress and fever would arise. For one who dwells overcoming an arisen bad friendship, those taints of distress and fever do not arise. This, indeed, bhikkhus, is the reason a bhikkhu should dwell repeatedly overcoming any arisen gain; any arisen lack of gain… any arisen fame… any arisen lack of fame… any arisen honor… any arisen lack of honor… any arisen evil desires… and any arisen bad friendship.”

“Tasmātiha, bhikkhave, evaṃ sikkhitabbaṃ – uppannaṃ [P 4.0166] lābhaṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya viharissāma, uppannaṃ alābhaṃ…pe… uppannaṃ yasaṃ… uppannaṃ ayasaṃ… uppannaṃ sakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ asakkāraṃ… uppannaṃ pāpicchataṃ… uppannaṃ pāpamittataṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya viharissāmāti. Evañhi vo, bhikkhave, sikkhitabba”nti.

“Therefore, bhikkhus, you should train thus: ‘We will dwell, repeatedly overcoming any arisen gain; any arisen lack of gain… any arisen fame… any arisen lack of fame… any arisen honor… any arisen lack of honor… any arisen evil desires… and any arisen bad friendship.’ Indeed, bhikkhus, thus should you train.”

“Ettāvatā, bhante uttara, manussesu catasso parisā – bhikkhū, bhikkhuniyo, upāsakā, upāsikāyo. Nāyaṃ dhammapariyāyo kismiñci upaṭṭhito patiṭṭhito (sī. syā.). Uggaṇhatu, bhante, āyasmā uttaro imaṃ dhammapariyāyaṃ. Pariyāpuṇātu, bhante, āyasmā uttaro imaṃ dhammapariyāyaṃ. Dhāretu, bhante, āyasmā uttaro imaṃ dhammapariyāyaṃ. Atthasaṃhito ayaṃ, bhante, dhammapariyāyo ādibrahmacariyako”ti ādibrahmacariyiko (sī. ka.). Aṭṭhamaṃ.

“Venerable Uttara, among humans there are these four assemblies: bhikkhus, bhikkhunīs, male lay followers, and female lay followers. This exposition of the Dhamma is not yet established among any of them. Venerable sir, may the Venerable Uttara learn this exposition of the Dhamma. Venerable sir, may the Venerable Uttara master this exposition of the Dhamma. Venerable sir, may the Venerable Uttara retain this exposition of the Dhamma. For, venerable sir, this exposition of the Dhamma is connected with benefit and is fundamental to the holy life.” The Eighth.

9. Nandasuttaṃ

9. The Discourse concerning Nanda

9. “‘Kulaputto’ti, bhikkhave, nandaṃ sammā vadamāno vadeyya. ‘Balavā’ti, bhikkhave, nandaṃ sammā vadamāno vadeyya. ‘Pāsādiko’ti, bhikkhave, nandaṃ sammā vadamāno vadeyya. ‘Tibbarāgo’ti, bhikkhave, nandaṃ sammā vadamāno vadeyya. Kimaññatra, bhikkhave, nando indriyesu guttadvāro, bhojane mattaññū, jāgariyaṃ anuyutto, satisampajaññena samannāgato, yehi yena (ka.) nando sakkoti paripuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ carituṃ! Tatridaṃ, bhikkhave, nandassa indriyesu guttadvāratāya hoti. Sace[P 4.0167], bhikkhave, nandassa puratthimā disā āloketabbā hoti, sabbaṃ cetasā samannāharitvā [T 4.0169] nando puratthimaṃ disaṃ āloketi – ‘evaṃ me puratthimaṃ disaṃ ālokayato nābhijjhādomanassā pāpakā akusalā dhammā anvāssavissantī’ti. Itiha tattha sampajāno hoti.

9. “Bhikkhus, if one were to speak rightly of Nanda, one would say he is ‘a clansman.’ Bhikkhus, if one were to speak rightly of Nanda, one would say he is ‘strong.’ Bhikkhus, if one were to speak rightly of Nanda, one would say he is ‘pleasing.’ Bhikkhus, if one were to speak rightly of Nanda, one would say he is ‘of intense passion.’ What else is there, Bhikkhus, but that Nanda guards the doors of his senses, knows moderation in food, is devoted to wakefulness, and is endowed with mindfulness and clear comprehension, by which Nanda is able to practice the complete and pure holy life? In this regard, Bhikkhus, this is Nanda's guarding of the doors of the senses. If, Bhikkhus, the eastern direction is to be looked at by Nanda, having attended to the whole matter with his mind, Nanda looks at the eastern direction, thinking: ‘As I am looking at the eastern direction in this way, covetousness and displeasure, evil, unwholesome states, will not assail me.’ Thus he is clearly comprehending in that matter.

“Sace[V 3.0016][M 3.0015], bhikkhave, nandassa pacchimā disā āloketabbā hoti…pe… uttarā disā āloketabbā hoti… dakkhiṇā disā āloketabbā hoti… uddhaṃ ulloketabbā hoti… adho oloketabbā hoti… anudisā anuviloketabbā hoti, sabbaṃ cetasā samannāharitvā nando anudisaṃ anuviloketi – ‘evaṃ me anudisaṃ anuvilokayato nābhijjhādomanassā pāpakā akusalā dhammā anvāssavissantī’ti. Itiha tattha sampajāno hoti. Idaṃ kho, bhikkhave, nandassa indriyesu guttadvāratāya hoti.

“If, Bhikkhus, the western direction is to be looked at by Nanda… and so forth… if an intermediate direction is to be looked at obliquely, having attended to the whole matter with his mind, Nanda looks obliquely at the intermediate direction, thinking: ‘As I am looking obliquely at an intermediate direction in this way, covetousness and displeasure, evil, unwholesome states, will not assail me.’ Thus he is clearly comprehending in that matter. This, indeed, Bhikkhus, is Nanda's guarding of the doors of the senses.

“Tatridaṃ, bhikkhave, nandassa bhojane mattaññutāya hoti. Idha, bhikkhave, nando paṭisaṅkhā yoniso āhāraṃ āhāreti – ‘neva davāya na madāya na maṇḍanāya na vibhūsanāya, yāvadeva imassa kāyassa ṭhitiyā yāpanāya vihiṃsūparatiyā brahmacariyānuggahāya iti purāṇañca vedanaṃ paṭihaṅkhāmi, navañca vedanaṃ na uppādessāmi, yātrā ca me bhavissati anavajjatā ca phāsuvihāro cā’ti. Idaṃ kho, bhikkhave, nandassa bhojane mattaññutāya hoti.

“In this regard, Bhikkhus, this is Nanda's moderation in food. Here, Bhikkhus, Nanda, reflecting wisely, partakes of food: ‘Not for amusement, not for intoxication, not for adornment, not for beautification, but only for the endurance and maintenance of this body, for the cessation of affliction, and for the support of the holy life. Thus I shall ward off the old feeling and not arouse a new feeling, and I shall maintain myself, be blameless, and dwell at ease.’ This, indeed, Bhikkhus, is Nanda's moderation in food.

“Tatridaṃ, bhikkhave, nandassa jāgariyānuyogasmiṃ hoti. Idha[P 4.0168], bhikkhave, nando divasaṃ caṅkamena nisajjāya āvaraṇīyehi dhammehi cittaṃ parisodheti; rattiyā paṭhamaṃ yāmaṃ caṅkamena nisajjāya āvaraṇīyehi dhammehi cittaṃ parisodheti[T 4.0170]; rattiyā majjhimaṃ yāmaṃ dakkhiṇena passena sīhaseyyaṃ kappeti pāde pādaṃ accādhāya sato sampajāno uṭṭhānasaññaṃ manasi karitvā; rattiyā pacchimaṃ yāmaṃ paccuṭṭhāya caṅkamena nisajjāya āvaraṇīyehi dhammehi cittaṃ parisodheti. Idaṃ kho, bhikkhave, nandassa jāgariyānuyogasmiṃ hoti.

“In this regard, Bhikkhus, this is Nanda's devotion to wakefulness. Here, Bhikkhus, during the day, Nanda purifies his mind of hindering states by walking back and forth and by sitting. In the first watch of the night, he purifies his mind of hindering states by walking back and forth and by sitting. In the middle watch of the night, he lies down on his right side in the lion’s posture, with one foot placed on the other, mindful and clearly comprehending, having attended to the perception of rising. In the last watch of the night, having risen, he purifies his mind of hindering states by walking back and forth and by sitting. This, indeed, Bhikkhus, is Nanda's devotion to wakefulness.

“Tatridaṃ, bhikkhave, nandassa satisampajaññasmiṃ hoti. Idha, bhikkhave, nandassa viditā vedanā uppajjanti, viditā upaṭṭhahanti, viditā abbhatthaṃ gacchanti; viditā saññā…pe… viditā vitakkā…pe… abbhatthaṃ gacchanti. Idaṃ kho, bhikkhave, nandassa satisampajaññasmiṃ hoti.

“In this regard, Bhikkhus, this is Nanda's mindfulness and clear comprehension. Here, Bhikkhus, for Nanda, feelings arise as known, are present as known, and cease as known; perceptions… and so on… thoughts arise as known, are present as known, and cease as known. This, indeed, Bhikkhus, is Nanda's mindfulness and clear comprehension.

“Kimaññatra, bhikkhave, nando indriyesu guttadvāro, bhojane mattaññū, jāgariyaṃ anuyutto[V 3.0017], satisampajaññena samannāgato, yehi nando sakkoti paripuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ caritu”nti! Navamaṃ.

“What else is there, Bhikkhus, but that Nanda guards the doors of his senses, knows moderation in food, is devoted to wakefulness, and is endowed with mindfulness and clear comprehension, by which Nanda is able to practice the complete and pure holy life?” The Ninth.

10. Kāraṇḍavasuttaṃ

10. The Kāraṇḍava Sutta

10. Ekaṃ [M 3.0016] samayaṃ bhagavā campāyaṃ viharati gaggarāya pokkharaṇiyā tīre. Tena kho pana samayena bhikkhū bhikkhuṃ āpattiyā codenti. So bhikkhu bhikkhūhi āpattiyā codiyamāno aññenāññaṃ paṭicarati, bahiddhā kathaṃ apanāmeti, kopañca dosañca appaccayañca pātukaroti.

10. At one time the Blessed One was dwelling at Campā on the bank of the Gaggarā Lotus Pond. At that time, monks were accusing a certain monk of an offense. That monk, being accused of an offense by the monks, behaved evasively, diverted the talk to external matters, and displayed anger, ill will, and displeasure.

Atha kho bhagavā bhikkhū āmantesi – “niddhamathetaṃ[P 4.0169], bhikkhave, puggalaṃ; niddhamathetaṃ, bhikkhave, puggalaṃ. Apaneyyeso apaneyyo so (sī.), apaneyyo (syā.), bhikkhave, puggalo. Kiṃ vo tena paraputtena visodhitena kiṃ voparaputto viheṭhiyati (sī.), kiṃ paraputto viheṭheti (syā.), kiṃ vo paraputtā viheṭheti (pī.), kiṃ so paraputto visodheti (ka.)! Idha[T 4.0171], bhikkhave, ekaccassa puggalassa tādisaṃyeva hoti abhikkantaṃ paṭikkantaṃ ālokitaṃ vilokitaṃ samiñjitaṃ pasāritaṃ saṅghāṭipattacīvaradhāraṇaṃ, seyyathāpi aññesaṃ bhaddakānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ – yāvassa bhikkhū āpattiṃ na passanti. Yato ca khvassa bhikkhū āpattiṃ passanti, tamenaṃ evaṃ jānanti – ‘samaṇadūsīvāyaṃ samaṇarūpī (ka.) samaṇapalāpo samaṇakāraṇḍavo’ti samaṇakaraṇḍavoti (ka.). Tamenaṃ iti viditvā bahiddhā nāsenti. Taṃ kissa hetu? Mā aññe bhaddake bhikkhū dūsesī”ti!

Then the Blessed One addressed the monks: “Drive out this person, monks! Drive out this person, monks! This person is to be removed, monks. What is this son of another to you, to be purified? Here, monks, for a certain person, his going forward and returning, looking ahead and looking aside, bending and stretching, and wearing of the double-layered robe, bowl, and robes are just the same as that of other virtuous monks—for as long as the monks do not see his offense. But when the monks do see his offense, they then know him thus: ‘This one is a corrupter of ascetics, chaff among ascetics, refuse among ascetics!’ Having known him thus, they banish him. For what reason? ‘May he not corrupt other virtuous monks!’”

“Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, sampanne yavakaraṇe yavadūsī yavarūpī (ka.) jāyetha yavapalāpo yavakāraṇḍavoti. Tassa tādisaṃyeva mūlaṃ hoti, seyyathāpi aññesaṃ bhaddakānaṃ yavānaṃ; tādisaṃyeva nāḷaṃ hoti, seyyathāpi aññesaṃ bhaddakānaṃ yavānaṃ; tādisaṃyeva pattaṃ hoti, seyyathāpi aññesaṃ bhaddakānaṃ yavānaṃ – yāvassa sīsaṃ na nibbattati. Yato ca khvassa sīsaṃ nibbattati, tamenaṃ evaṃ jānanti – ‘yavadūsīvāyaṃ yavapalāpo yavakāraṇḍavo’ti[P 4.0170]. Tamenaṃ iti viditvā samūlaṃ uppāṭetvā bahiddhā yavakaraṇassa chaḍḍenti. Taṃ kissa hetu? Mā aññe bhaddake yave dūsesīti!

“Just as, monks, in a thriving barley field, a barley-spoiler might grow, a barley-chaff weed, a barley-refuse weed. It has the very same root as the other good barley plants; it has the very same stalk as the other good barley plants; it has the very same leaves as the other good barley plants—for as long as its head has not appeared. But when its head does appear, they then know it thus: ‘This is a barley-spoiler, a barley-chaff weed, a barley-refuse weed!’ Having known it thus, they uproot it completely and discard it outside the barley field. For what reason? ‘May it not spoil the other good barley plants!’”

“Evamevaṃ kho, bhikkhave, idhekaccassa puggalassa tādisaṃyeva hoti abhikkantaṃ paṭikkantaṃ ālokitaṃ vilokitaṃ samiñjitaṃ pasāritaṃ saṅghāṭipattacīvaradhāraṇaṃ, seyyathāpi aññesaṃ [V 3.0018] bhaddakānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ – yāvassa [M 3.0017] bhikkhū āpattiṃ na passanti. Yato ca khvassa bhikkhū āpattiṃ passanti, tamenaṃ evaṃ jānanti – ‘samaṇadūsīvāyaṃ samaṇapalāpo samaṇakāraṇḍavo’ti. Tamenaṃ iti viditvā bahiddhā nāsenti. Taṃ kissa [T 4.0172] hetu? Mā aññe bhaddake bhikkhū dūsesīti.

“So too, monks, here for a certain person, his going forward and returning, looking ahead and looking aside, bending and stretching, and wearing of the double-layered robe, bowl, and robes are just the same as that of other virtuous monks—for as long as the monks do not see his offense. But when the monks do see his offense, they then know him thus: ‘This one is a corrupter of ascetics, chaff among ascetics, refuse among ascetics!’ Having known him thus, they banish him. For what reason? ‘May he not corrupt other virtuous monks!’”

“Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, mahato dhaññarāsissa phuṇamānassa vuyhamānassa (sī. pī.), phusayamānassa (syā.), punamānassa (?) tattha yāni tāni dhaññāni daḷhāni sāravantāni tāni ekamantaṃ puñjaṃ hoti, yāni pana tāni dhaññāni dubbalāni palāpāni tāni vāto ekamantaṃ apavahati apakassati (sī.). Tamenaṃ sāmikā sammajjaniṃ gahetvā bhiyyosomattāya apasammajjanti. Taṃ kissa hetu? Mā aññe bhaddake dhaññe dūsesīti! Evamevaṃ kho, bhikkhave, idhekaccassa puggalassa tādisaṃyeva hoti abhikkantaṃ paṭikkantaṃ ālokitaṃ vilokitaṃ samiñjitaṃ pasāritaṃ saṅghāṭipattacīvaradhāraṇaṃ, seyyathāpi aññesaṃ bhaddakānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ – yāvassa bhikkhū āpattiṃ na passanti. Yato ca khvassa bhikkhū āpattiṃ passanti, tamenaṃ evaṃ jānanti [P 4.0171] – ‘samaṇadūsīvāyaṃ samaṇapalāpo samaṇakāraṇḍavo’ti. Tamenaṃ iti viditvā bahiddhā nāsenti. Taṃ kissa hetu? Mā aññe bhaddake bhikkhū dūsesīti.

“Just as, monks, when a great heap of grain is being winnowed, the grains that are firm and substantial form a pile on one side, while the grains that are weak and are chaff are carried away to one side by the wind. The owners, taking a broom, sweep them away even more. For what reason? ‘May they not spoil the other good grains!’ So too, monks, here for a certain person, his going forward and returning, looking ahead and looking aside, bending and stretching, and wearing of the double-layered robe, bowl, and robes are just the same as that of other virtuous monks—for as long as the monks do not see his offense. But when the monks do see his offense, they then know him thus: ‘This one is a corrupter of ascetics, chaff among ascetics, refuse among ascetics!’ Having known him thus, they banish him. For what reason? ‘May he not corrupt other virtuous monks!’”

“Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, puriso udapānapanāḷiyatthiko tiṇhaṃ kuṭhāriṃ kudhāriṃ (syā. kaṃ. ka.) ādāya vanaṃ paviseyya. So yaṃ yadeva rukkhaṃ kuṭhāripāsena ākoṭeyya tattha yāni tāni rukkhāni daḷhāni sāravantāni tāni kuṭhāripāsena ākoṭitāni kakkhaḷaṃ paṭinadanti; yāni pana tāni rukkhāni antopūtīni avassutāni kasambujātāni tāni kuṭhāripāsena ākoṭitāni daddaraṃ paṭinadanti. Tamenaṃ mūle chindati, mūle chinditvā agge chindati, agge chinditvā anto suvisodhitaṃ visodheti, anto suvisodhitaṃ visodhetvā udapānapanāḷiṃ yojeti. Evamevaṃ kho, bhikkhave[T 4.0173], idhekaccassa puggalassa tādisaṃyeva hoti abhikkantaṃ paṭikkantaṃ ālokitaṃ vilokitaṃ samiñjitaṃ pasāritaṃ saṅghāṭipattacīvaradhāraṇaṃ, seyyathāpi aññesaṃ bhaddakānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ – yāvassa bhikkhū āpattiṃ na passanti. Yato ca khvassa bhikkhū āpattiṃ passanti, tamenaṃ evaṃ [M 3.0018] jānanti – ‘samaṇadūsīvāyaṃ samaṇapalāpo samaṇakāraṇḍavo’ti. Tamenaṃ iti viditvā bahiddhā nāsenti. Taṃ kissa hetu? Mā aññe bhaddake bhikkhū dūsesī”ti.

“Just as, monks, a man needing a water conduit might take a sharp axe and enter a forest. Whatever tree he might tap with the back of the axe—those trees that are firm and have heartwood, when tapped with the back of the axe, resound with a hard sound; but those trees that are rotten within, waterlogged, and have become rubbish, when tapped with the back of the axe, resound with a dull thud. He cuts that one at the root; having cut it at the root, he cuts it at the top; having cut it at the top, he thoroughly cleans out the inside. Having thoroughly cleaned out the inside, he installs it as a water conduit. So too, monks, here for a certain person, his going forward and returning, looking ahead and looking aside, bending and stretching, and wearing of the double-layered robe, bowl, and robes are just the same as that of other virtuous monks—for as long as the monks do not see his offense. But when the monks do see his offense, they then know him thus: ‘This one is a corrupter of ascetics, chaff among ascetics, refuse among ascetics!’ Having known him thus, they banish him. For what reason? ‘May he not corrupt other virtuous monks!’”

“Saṃvāsāyaṃ [V 3.0019][P 4.0172] vijānātha, pāpiccho kodhano iti;

Makkhī thambhī paḷāsī ca, issukī maccharī saṭho.

“Recognize him through living together: he has evil desires and is angry; he is a denigrator, stubborn, and spiteful; envious, miserly, and deceitful.

“Santavāco janavati, samaṇo viya bhāsati;

Raho karoti karaṇaṃ, pāpadiṭṭhi anādaro.

“Sweet-spoken in an assembly, he speaks like an ascetic; he does the deed in secret, being of wrong view and disrespectful.

“Saṃsappī ca musāvādī, taṃ viditvā yathātathaṃ;

Sabbe samaggā hutvāna, abhinibbajjayātha abhinibbijjayetha (ka.) naṃ.

“He is treacherous and a liar; having known him as he really is, all of you, being united, should drive him out.

“Kāraṇḍavaṃ karaṇḍavaṃ (ka.) su. ni. 283 passitabbaṃ niddhamatha, kasambuṃ apakassatha avakassatha (ka.);

Tato palāpe vāhetha, assamaṇe samaṇamānine.

“Drive out the refuse, drag away the scum; then get rid of the chaff—those who are not ascetics but consider themselves ascetics.

“Niddhamitvāna pāpicche, pāpaācāragocare;

Suddhāsuddhehi saṃvāsaṃ, kappayavho patissatā;

Tato samaggā nipakā, dukkhassantaṃ karissathā”ti. dasamaṃ;

“Having driven out those of evil desires, whose resort is evil conduct, you pure ones, being mindful, should associate with the pure. Then, united and wise, you will make an end of suffering.” The Tenth Sutta is finished.

Mettāvaggo paṭhamo.

The First Chapter on Loving-Kindness.

Tassuddānaṃ –

The summary verse for this is:

Mettaṃ [T 4.0174] paññā ca dve piyā, dve lokā dve vipattiyo;

Devadatto ca uttaro, nando kāraṇḍavena cāti.

Loving-kindness, Wisdom, two on Being Dear, two on Worldly Conditions, Devadatta, Uttara, Nanda, and Kāraṇḍava.

2. Mahāvaggo

2. The Great Chapter

1. Verañjasuttaṃ

1. The Verañja Sutta

11. pārā. 1 ādayo Evaṃ [V 3.0020] me sutaṃ – ekaṃ samayaṃ bhagavā verañjāyaṃ viharati naḷerupucimandamūle. Atha kho verañjo brāhmaṇo [P 4.0173] yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavatā saddhiṃ sammodi. Sammodanīyaṃ kathaṃ sāraṇīyaṃ sārāṇīyaṃ (sī. syā. kaṃ. pī.) vītisāretvā [M 3.0019] ekamantaṃ nisīdi. Ekamantaṃ nisinno kho verañjo brāhmaṇo bhagavantaṃ etadavoca –

11. Thus have I heard. At one time the Blessed One was dwelling in Verañja at the root of Naḷeru's Pucimanda tree. Then the brahmin Verañja approached the Blessed One; having approached, he exchanged joyful greetings with the Blessed One. Having concluded a cordial and memorable conversation, he sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the brahmin Verañja said this to the Blessed One:

“Sutaṃ metaṃ, bho gotama – ‘na samaṇo gotamo brāhmaṇe jiṇṇe vuḍḍhe mahallake addhagate vayoanuppatte abhivādeti vā paccuṭṭheti vā āsanena vā nimantetī’ti. Tayidaṃ, bho gotama, tatheva. Na hi bhavaṃ gotamo brāhmaṇe jiṇṇe vuḍḍhe mahallake addhagate vayoanuppatte abhivādeti vā paccuṭṭheti vā āsanena vā nimanteti. Tayidaṃ, bho gotama, na sampannamevā”ti. “Nāhaṃ taṃ, brāhmaṇa, passāmi sadevake loke samārake sabrahmake sassamaṇabrāhmaṇiyā pajāya sadevamanussāya yamahaṃ abhivādeyyaṃ vā paccuṭṭheyyaṃ vā āsanena vā nimanteyyaṃ. Yañhi, brāhmaṇa, tathāgato abhivādeyya [T 4.0175] vā paccuṭṭheyya vā āsanena vā nimanteyya, muddhāpi tassa vipateyyā”ti.

“Venerable Gotama, I have heard this: ‘The ascetic Gotama does not pay homage to, rise up for, or invite with a seat those brahmins who are old, aged, senior, advanced in years, and have reached the final stage of life.’ Venerable Gotama, that is indeed so. The Venerable Gotama does not pay homage to, rise up for, or invite with a seat those brahmins who are old, aged, senior, advanced in years, and have reached the final stage of life. Venerable Gotama, this is not at all proper.” “Brahmin, in this world with its devas, Māra, and Brahmā, in this generation with its ascetics and brahmins, with its devas and humans, I do not see anyone whom I should pay homage to, rise up for, or invite with a seat. For, brahmin, if the Tathāgata were to pay homage to, rise up for, or invite anyone with a seat, that person’s head would split apart.”

“Arasarūpo bhavaṃ gotamo”ti! “Atthi khvesa, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘arasarūpo samaṇo gotamo’ti. Ye te, brāhmaṇa, rūparasā saddarasā gandharasā rasarasā phoṭṭhabbarasā, te tathāgatassa pahīnā ucchinnamūlā tālāvatthukatā anabhāvaṃkatā anabhāvakatā (sī. pī.) āyatiṃ anuppādadhammā. Ayaṃ kho, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘arasarūpo samaṇo gotamo’ti, no ca kho yaṃ tvaṃ sandhāya vadesī”ti vadesi (sī. ka.).

“The Venerable Gotama is without taste!” “There is indeed, brahmin, a way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is without taste.’ Brahmin, any tastes for visible forms, tastes for sounds, tastes for smells, tastes for flavors, tastes for tangible objects—these have been abandoned by the Tathāgata, cut off at the root, made like a palm-stump, brought to non-existence, and are of a nature not to arise in the future. This, brahmin, is the way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is without taste,’ but it is not in the sense that you mean.”

“Nibbhogo [P 4.0174] bhavaṃ gotamo”ti! “Atthi khvesa, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena [V 3.0021] sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘nibbhogo samaṇo gotamo’ti. Ye te, brāhmaṇa, rūpabhogā saddabhogā gandhabhogā rasabhogā phoṭṭhabbabhogā, te tathāgatassa pahīnā ucchinnamūlā tālāvatthukatā anabhāvaṃkatā āyatiṃ anuppādadhammā. Ayaṃ kho, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘nibbhogo samaṇo gotamo’ti, no ca kho yaṃ tvaṃ sandhāya vadesī”ti.

“The Venerable Gotama is without enjoyment!” “There is indeed, brahmin, a way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is without enjoyment.’ Brahmin, any enjoyments of visible forms, enjoyments of sounds, enjoyments of smells, enjoyments of flavors, enjoyments of tangible objects—these have been abandoned by the Tathāgata, cut off at the root, made like a palm-stump, brought to non-existence, and are of a nature not to arise in the future. This, brahmin, is the way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is without enjoyment,’ but it is not in the sense that you mean.”

“Akiriyavādo [M 3.0020] bhavaṃ gotamo”ti! “Atthi khvesa, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘akiriyavādo samaṇo gotamo’ti. Ahañhi, brāhmaṇa, akiriyaṃ vadāmi kāyaduccaritassa vacīduccaritassa manoduccaritassa; anekavihitānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ akiriyaṃ [T 4.0176] vadāmi. Ayaṃ kho, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘akiriyavādo samaṇo gotamo’ti, no ca kho yaṃ tvaṃ sandhāya vadesī”ti.

“The Venerable Gotama is a proponent of non-action!” “There is indeed, brahmin, a way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is a proponent of non-action.’ For I, brahmin, teach the non-doing of bodily misconduct, verbal misconduct, and mental misconduct; I teach the non-doing of many kinds of evil, unwholesome states. This, brahmin, is the way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is a proponent of non-action,’ but it is not in the sense that you mean.”

“Ucchedavādo bhavaṃ gotamo”ti! “Atthi khvesa, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘ucchedavādo samaṇo gotamo’ti. Ahañhi, brāhmaṇa, ucchedaṃ vadāmi rāgassa dosassa mohassa; anekavihitānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ ucchedaṃ vadāmi. Ayaṃ kho, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘ucchedavādo samaṇo gotamo’ti, no ca kho yaṃ tvaṃ sandhāya vadesī”ti.

“The Venerable Gotama is a proponent of annihilation!” “There is indeed, brahmin, a way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is a proponent of annihilation.’ For I, brahmin, teach the cutting off of greed, hatred, and delusion; I teach the cutting off of many kinds of evil, unwholesome states. This, brahmin, is the way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is a proponent of annihilation,’ but it is not in the sense that you mean.”

“Jegucchī bhavaṃ gotamo”ti! “Atthi khvesa, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘jegucchī samaṇo gotamo’ti. Ahañhi, brāhmaṇa, jigucchāmi kāyaduccaritena vacīduccaritena manoduccaritena; jigucchāmi anekavihitānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ [P 4.0175] samāpattiyā. Ayaṃ kho, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘jegucchī samaṇo gotamo’ti, no ca kho yaṃ tvaṃ sandhāya vadesī”ti.

“The Venerable Gotama is fastidious!” “There is indeed, brahmin, a way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is fastidious.’ For I, brahmin, am disgusted by bodily misconduct, verbal misconduct, and mental misconduct; I am disgusted by the attainment of many kinds of evil, unwholesome states. This, brahmin, is the way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is fastidious,’ but it is not in the sense that you mean.”

“Venayiko bhavaṃ gotamo”ti! “Atthi khvesa, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘venayiko samaṇo gotamo’ti. Ahañhi, brāhmaṇa, vinayāya dhammaṃ desemi rāgassa dosassa mohassa; anekavihitānaṃ [T 4.0177] pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ vinayāya dhammaṃ [V 3.0022] desemi. Ayaṃ kho, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘venayiko samaṇo gotamo’ti, no ca kho yaṃ tvaṃ sandhāya vadesī”ti.

“The Venerable Gotama is a destroyer!” “There is indeed, brahmin, a way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is a destroyer.’ For I, brahmin, teach the Dhamma for the removal of greed, hatred, and delusion; I teach the Dhamma for the removal of many kinds of evil, unwholesome states. This, brahmin, is the way in which one, speaking rightly, could say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is a destroyer,’ but it is not in the sense that you mean.”

“Tapassī bhavaṃ gotamo”ti! “Atthi khvesa, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘tapassī samaṇo gotamo’ti. Tapanīyāhaṃ[M 3.0021], brāhmaṇa, pāpake akusale dhamme vadāmi kāyaduccaritaṃ vacīduccaritaṃ manoduccaritaṃ. Yassa kho, brāhmaṇa, tapanīyā pāpakā akusalā dhammā pahīnā ucchinnamūlā tālāvatthukatā anabhāvaṃkatā āyatiṃ anuppādadhammā, tamahaṃ ‘tapassī’ti vadāmi. Tathāgatassa kho, brāhmaṇa, tapanīyā pāpakā akusalā dhammā pahīnā ucchinnamūlā tālāvatthukatā anabhāvaṃkatā āyatiṃ anuppādadhammā. Ayaṃ kho, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘tapassī samaṇo gotamo’ti, no ca kho yaṃ tvaṃ sandhāya vadesī”ti.

“The venerable Gotama is an ascetic!” “There is indeed, brahmin, a way in which one speaking rightly might say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is an ascetic.’ I declare, brahmin, that evil, unwholesome states—misconduct of body, speech, and mind—are tormenting. Whoever, brahmin, has abandoned these tormenting, evil, unwholesome states, cut them off at the root, made them like a palm-stump, brought them to non-existence, so that they are of a nature not to arise in the future—him I call an ‘ascetic.’ For the Tathāgata, brahmin, these tormenting, evil, unwholesome states have been abandoned, cut off at the root, made like a palm-stump, brought to non-existence, and are of a nature not to arise in the future. This, brahmin, is the way in which one speaking rightly might say of me: ‘The recluse Gotama is an ascetic,’ but it is not in the sense that you mean.”

“Apagabbho bhavaṃ gotamo”ti! “Atthi khvesa, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyo, yena maṃ pariyāyena sammā vadamāno vadeyya – ‘apagabbho samaṇo gotamo’ti. Yassa kho, brāhmaṇa, āyatiṃ gabbhaseyyā punabbhavābhinibbatti pahīnā ucchinnamūlā tālāvatthukatā anabhāvaṃkatā āyatiṃ anuppādadhammā[P 4.0176], tamahaṃ ‘apagabbho’ti vadāmi. Tathāgatassa kho, brāhmaṇa[T 4.0178], āyatiṃ gabbhaseyyā punabbhavābhinibbatti pahīnā ucchinnamūlā tālāvatthukatā anabhāvaṃkatā āyatiṃ anuppādadhammā.
